Arithmetic volumes of unitary Shimura varieties Thanks: J.B. was supported in part by the LOEWE research unit USAG and by the DFG Collaborative Research Centre TRR 326 “Geometry and Arithmetic of Uniformized Structures”, project number 444845124. Thanks: B.H. was supported in part by NSF grants DMS-1801905 and DMS-2101636.
Jan Hendrik Bruinier, Benjamin Howard

Abstract
. The integral model of a GU ⁡ ( n − 1 , 1 ) \mathrm{GU}(n-1,1) Shimura variety carries a universal abelian scheme over it, and the dual top exterior power of its Lie algebra carries a natural hermitian metric. We express the arithmetic volume of this metrized line bundle, defined as an iterated self-intersection in the arithmetic Chow ring, in terms of logarithmic derivatives of Dirichlet L L -functions. We also determine the arithmetic volumes of Kudla-Rapoport divisors and relate them to coefficients of Eisenstein series.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 11G18, 14G35

Chapter 1
Introduction

The explicit calculation of arithmetic volumes of Shimura varieties began with the work of Kühn
[
Kü01
]
and Bost (independently, in unpublished work). Over the moduli stack
\mathcal{X}\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
of elliptic curves there is a line bundle of weight one modular forms, characterized as the dual Lie algebra of the universal elliptic curve.
This line bundle carries a natural hermitian metric, and so determines a class in the codimension one arithmetic Chow group of
\mathcal{X}
of Gillet-Soulé (as extended by Burgos-Kramer-Kühn). Working on a suitable compactification, Kühn and Bost computed the self-intersection multiplicity of this hermitian line bundle, and gave a simple formula for it in terms of the logarithmic derivative of the Riemann zeta function at
s=-1
.

More generally, if
\mathcal{X}
is an integral model of a PEL type (or even Hodge type) Shimura variety, then
\mathcal{X}
carries a
Hodge bundle
: the dual of the top exterior power of the Lie algebra of the universal abelian scheme over
\mathcal{X}
. The Hodge bundle again carries a natural hermitian metric, and one can ask if its arithmetic volume, defined as the
\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{X})
-fold self-intersection in the arithmetic Chow group, is again related to logarithmic derivatives of Dirichlet
L
-functions.

In some instances this is known.
If
\mathcal{X}
is the integral model of a quaternionic Shimura curve over
\mathbb{Q}
, the volume was computed by Kudla-Rapoport-Yang
[
KRY06
]
. Hilbert modular surfaces and the Siegel threefold were considered by Bruinier-Burgos-Kühn
[
BBGK07
]
and Jung-von Pippich
[
JVP
]
, respectively.
The arithmetic volumes of
\mathrm{GSpin}(n,2)
Shimura varieties, which include all of the above examples as special cases, were computed (up to a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combination of logarithms of certain bad primes) by Hörmann
[
Hö14
]
.
Most recently, Yuan
[
Yua
]
extended the above mentioned result of Kudla-Rapoport-Yang
(using completely different methods)
to all quaternionic Shimura curves over totally real fields.

In
[
FiMS18
]
Freixas and Sankaran study arithmetic intersections on a Shimura curve and on a twisted Hilbert modular surface over the same real quadratic base field. They relate the corresponding arithmetic volumes via the arithmetic Riemann-Roch Theorem and the Jacquet-Langlands correspondence.
Maillot and Rössler
[
MR18
]
employ the equivariant Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch Theorem in Arakelov geometry to derive general conjectures relating Chern classes of certain hermitian vector bundles in Arakelov geometry to logarithmic derivatives of Artin
L
-functions of number fields. See in particular Section 8 of
[
MR18
]
for a discussion of the relationship with some of the above results on arithmetic volumes of Shimura varieties.
Finally, we mention the work of Andreasson and Berman, who study arithmetic varieties for which the log canonical bundle (or its dual) with respect to a suitable divisor is relatively ample
[
AB24
]
. Under some stability condition, they equip this bundle with a Kähler-Einstein metric and study various properties of the corresponding arithmetic volume. In the special case when the arithmetic variety is
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}
they use their results to obtain different proofs of some of the above results for Shimura curves of genus zero, see Section 9 of
[
AB24
]
.

The present paper deals with the case of
\mathrm{GU}(n-1,1)
Shimura varieties.
The overall strategy is closely related to that of
[
BBGK07
]
and
[
Hö14
]
, insofar as it ultimately rests on the use of Borcherds products. However, Borcherds products will make no explicit appearance in this paper; all the arithmetic information we need from the theory has already been extracted in
[
BHK+20a
]
, and is recalled here in §
7.2
.

1.1.
The volume formulas

Let
{\bm{k}}\subset\mathbb{C}
be an imaginary quadratic field of odd discriminant

-D=\mathrm{disc}({\bm{k}}).

Given an integer
n\geq 1
, there is a regular Deligne-Mumford stack

\mathcal{M}_{(n-1,1)}\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}),

flat of relative dimension
n-1
,
parametrizing principally polarized abelian schemes
A
, endowed with an action
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}\to\operatorname{End}(A)
, and extra data encoding a signature
(n-1,1)
condition on
\operatorname{Lie}(A)
.
See §
4.1
for details.

This stack admits a decomposition

\mathcal{M}_{(n-1,1)}=\bigsqcup_{W}\mathcal{M}_{W}

into open and closed substacks indexed by strict similarity classes of relevant
{\bm{k}}
-hermitian spaces
(W,h)
of signature
(n-1,1)
, in such a way that the generic fiber of
\mathcal{M}_{W}
is a Shimura variety for the unitary similitude group
\mathrm{GU}(W)
.
Here
relevant
means that
W
contains an
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-lattice
\mathfrak{a}\subset W
that is self-dual, in the sense of (
1.5.1
).
The notion of strict similarity is defined in §
4.1
, but the important fact is that there is a dichotomy between the cases of
n
odd and
n
even.
If
n
is odd the relevant
W
form a single strict similarity class, and the disjoint union has a single term.
If
n
is even then strict similarity is equivalent to isometry, and the number of terms in the disjoint union is
2^{o(D)-1}
, where

o(D)=\#\{\mbox{prime divisors of }D\}.

Fix a relevant
W
as above, and denote by

\pi:A\to\mathcal{M}_{W}

the restriction to
\mathcal{M}_{W}
of the universal abelian scheme over
\mathcal{M}_{(n-1,1)}
.
Its relative dimension is
n=\operatorname{dim}(A)
, and its
metrized Hodge bundle

\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}}\in\widehat{\operatorname{Pic}}(\mathcal{M}_{W})

is the line bundle

\omega^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}}=\pi_{*}\Omega^{\operatorname{dim}(A)}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}}

endowed with the hermitian metric

(1.1.1)

\|s_{z}\|^{2}=\left|\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{\operatorname{dim}(A)}}\int_{A_{z}(\mathbb{C})}s_{z}\wedge\overline{s}_{z}\right|

for any
z\in\mathcal{M}_{W}(\mathbb{C})
and

s_{z}\in\omega^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W},z}\cong H^{0}(A_{z},\Omega^{\operatorname{dim}(A_{z})}_{A_{z}/\mathbb{C}}).

The stack
\mathcal{M}_{W}
has a canonical toroidal compactification
\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{W}
, with boundary a Cartier divisor smooth over
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
.
Although the Hodge bundle has a distinguished extension to the compactification, the metric on it does not extend smoothly. Instead, the metric is pre-log singular along the boundary, in the sense of Definition 1.20 of
[
BBGK07
]
.
The pre-log singular conditions allow us to view

(1.1.2)

\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}}\in\widehat{\operatorname{CH}}^{1}(\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{W},\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{pre}})

as a class in the codimension one arithmetic Chow group of Burgos-Kramer-Kühn
[
BGKK07
]
.

These arithmetic Chow groups come with intersection pairings, and distinguished linear functionals in codimensions
n-1
and
n
. The first of these linear functionals is the
complex degree
, denoted

\deg_{\mathbb{C}}:\widehat{\operatorname{CH}}^{n-1}(\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{W},\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{pre}})\to\mathbb{Q},

defined by first sending an arithmetic cycle class to its image in the Chow group
\operatorname{CH}^{n-1}(\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{W/\mathbb{C}})
of
0
-cycles on the complex fiber, and then taking the degree in the usual sense.
The second is the more subtle
arithmetic degree
, denoted

\widehat{\deg}:\widehat{\operatorname{CH}}^{n}(\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{W},\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{pre}})\to\mathbb{R}.

We define the
complex volume

\operatorname{vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}})=\deg_{\mathbb{C}}\big(\underbrace{\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}}\cdots\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}}}_{n-1\ \mathrm{times}}\big)

and the

arithmetic volume

\widehat{\operatorname{vol}}(\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}})=\widehat{\deg}\big(\underbrace{\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}}\cdots\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}}}_{n\ \mathrm{times}}\big)

as the complex and arithmetic degrees of the
(n-1)
-fold and
n
-fold iterated intersections, respectively.

Our main result is the calculation of these complex and arithmetic volumes in terms of Dirichlet
L
-functions.
To state it, for any place
\ell\leq\infty
denote by

\operatorname{inv}_{\ell}(W)=(\det W,-D)_{\ell}\in\{\pm 1\}

the local invariant of
W
, where the right hand side is the usual Hilbert symbol.
Our assumption that
W
contains a self-dual
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-lattice is equivalent to the condition that
\mathrm{inv}_{\ell}(W)=1
for all finite primes
\ell\nmid D
. As
\operatorname{inv}_{\infty}(W)=-1
, and the product of all local invariants is
1
, we obtain

(1.1.3)

\prod_{\ell\mid D}\mathrm{inv}_{\ell}(W)=-1.

The quadratic Dirichlet character determined by
{\bm{k}}/\mathbb{Q}
is denoted

\varepsilon:(\mathbb{Z}/D\mathbb{Z})^{\times}\to\{\pm 1\}.

For an integer
k\geq 1
set

(1.1.4)

\mathbf{a}_{k}(s)=\frac{D^{k/2}\Gamma(s+k)L(2s+k,\varepsilon^{k})}{2^{k}\pi^{s+k}},

where, if
k
is even, we understand
L(s,\varepsilon^{k})=\zeta(s)
. Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{W}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{a}_{1}(s)\cdots\mathbf{a}_{n}(s)

(1.1.5)

\displaystyle\quad\times\begin{cases}\prod_{\ell\mid D}\big(1+\left(\frac{-1}{\ell}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\mathrm{inv}_{\ell}(W)\ell^{-s-\frac{n}{2}}\big)&\mbox{if $n$ is even}\\
1&\mbox{if $n$ is odd.}\end{cases}

If
n
is odd there is no dependence on
W
beyond its dimension.
We note that all
\mathbf{a}_{k}(0)
are positive rational numbers, and hence so is
\mathbf{A}_{W}(0)
.

The following is our main result. It appears in the text as Theorem
8.6.1
.

Theorem A
.

The metrized Hodge bundle has complex volume

\operatorname{vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}})=\mathbf{A}_{W}(0)\cdot\begin{cases}2^{n-1}&\mbox{if $n$ is odd }\\
2^{n-o(D)}&\mbox{if $n$ is even}\end{cases}

and arithmetic volume

\widehat{\operatorname{vol}}(\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}})=\left(2\frac{\mathbf{A}_{W}^{\prime}(0)}{\mathbf{A}_{W}(0)}-nC_{0}(n)+\log(D)\right)\cdot\operatorname{vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widehat{\omega}^{\mathrm{Hdg}}_{A/\mathcal{M}_{W}}),

where we have set

C_{0}(n)=2\log\left(\frac{4\pi e^{\gamma}}{\sqrt{D}}\right)+(n-4)\left(\frac{L^{\prime}(0,\varepsilon)}{L(0,\varepsilon)}+\frac{\log(D)}{2}\right).

Here
\gamma=-\Gamma^{\prime}(1)
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant.

Remark 1.1.1
.

Gross
[
Gro97
]
associates a motive to a reductive group over a field.
The
L
-function of the motive associated to
\mathrm{U}(W)
is essentially
\mathbf{a}_{1}(s)\cdots\mathbf{a}_{n}(s)
, which perhaps provides some explanation for why this particular product appears in the statement of Theorem
A
.

Remark 1.1.2
.

There is a slightly different way to express the meromorphic function
\mathbf{A}_{W}(s)
as a product.
Define

\mathbf{b}_{W,1}(s)=\mathbf{a}_{1}(s).

For
k\geq 2
even, set

\mathbf{b}_{W,k}(s)=\mathbf{a}_{k}(s)\prod_{\ell\mid D}\left(1+\left(\frac{-1}{\ell}\right)^{\frac{k}{2}}\mathrm{inv}_{\ell}(W)\ell^{-s-\frac{k}{2}}\right).

For
k\geq 3
odd, set

\mathbf{b}_{W,k}(s)=\mathbf{a}_{k}(s)\prod_{\ell\mid D}\left(1+\left(\frac{-1}{\ell}\right)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\mathrm{inv}_{\ell}(W)\ell^{-s+\frac{1-k}{2}}\right)^{-1}.

When
k>1
we have

\mathbf{b}_{W,k}(s)\mathbf{b}_{W,k+1}(s)=\mathbf{a}_{k}(s)\mathbf{a}_{k+1}(s)
,
which implies that

\mathbf{A}_{W}(s)=\mathbf{b}_{W,1}(s)\cdots\mathbf{b}_{W,n}(s).

1.2.
The generating series of divisors

Most of the calculations that go into the proof of Theorem
A
are not carried out on the
\mathrm{GU}(W)
Shimura variety
\mathcal{M}_{W}
, but rather on a slightly different Shimura variety.

Let us again fix a relevant
{\bm{k}}
-hermitian space
V
of signature
(n-1,1)
, and now assume
n\geq 2
.
We may present this hermitian space in the form

V\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\bm{k}}(W_{0},W),

where
W_{0}
and
W
are relevant
{\bm{k}}
-hermitian space of signature
(1,0)
and
(n-1,1)
, respectively (the right hand side carries a natural
{\bm{k}}
-hermitian form, and here we require that the isomorphism is an isometry).
Following
[
KR14
]
, we associate to
V
an open and closed substack

\mathcal{S}_{V}\subset\mathcal{M}_{W_{0}}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}})}\mathcal{M}_{W}.

As explained in §2 of
[
BHK+20a
]
, the generic fiber of
\mathcal{S}_{V}
is a Shimura variety for the subgroup
G\subset\mathrm{GU}(W_{0})\times\mathrm{GU}(W)
consisting of pairs for which the similitude factors are equal, and there is a natural surjection
G\to\mathrm{U}(V)
.
This allows one to view the moduli space
\mathcal{S}_{V}
as an approximation to the Shimura variety for the unitary group
\mathrm{U}(V)
, which is awkward to work with directly because it is of abelian type but not PEL type.

In §
5.1
we recall that the Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}_{V}
carries a family of Kudla-Rapoport divisors

\mathcal{Z}_{V}(m)\to\mathcal{S}_{V}

indexed by positive
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. It also carries a distinguished line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{V}
, called the
tautological bundle
or the
line bundle of weight one modular forms
, and an
exceptional divisor

\mathrm{Exc}_{V}\subset\mathcal{S}_{V}
that is proper and supported in characteristics
\ell\mid D
.

The main result of
[
BHK+20a
]
says that the Kudla-Rapoport divisors can be enhanced to arithmetic divisors

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}^{\mathrm{tot}}(m)=(\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}(m)+\mathcal{B}_{V}(m),\Phi_{V}(m))\in\widehat{\operatorname{CH}}^{1}(\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{V},\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{pre}})

(for
m>0
) in such a way that the formal generating series

\widehat{\phi}_{V}(\tau)=\sum_{m\geq 0}\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}^{\mathrm{tot}}(m)\cdot q^{m}\in\widehat{\operatorname{CH}}^{1}(\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{V},\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{pre}})[[q]]

is, at least when
n\geq 2
, a modular form. More precisely, it is the
q
-expansion of an element

\widehat{\phi}_{V}(\tau)\in M_{n}(\Gamma_{0}(D),\varepsilon^{n})\otimes\widehat{\operatorname{CH}}^{1}(\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{V},\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{pre}}).

Here
\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{V}
is the canonical toroidal compactification of
\mathcal{S}_{V}
,

\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}(m)\subset\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{V}
is the Zariski closure of
\mathcal{Z}_{V}(m)
,

\mathcal{B}_{V}(m)
is an explicit linear combination of boundary components, and
\Phi_{V}(m)
is a Green function for
\bar{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}(m)+\mathcal{B}_{V}(m)
constructed using the theory of regularized theta lifts. The constant term is defined by first endowing
\mathcal{L}_{V}
with a particular pre-log singular hermitian metric, and then setting

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}^{\mathrm{tot}}(0)=-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{V}+(\mathrm{Exc}_{V},-\log(D)).

The second term on the right is the exceptional divisor endowed with the constant Green function
-\log(D)
.

Given that the above generating series is modular, the arithmetic divisor

(1.2.1)

\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}2\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{V}-(\mathrm{Exc}_{V},0)\in\widehat{\operatorname{CH}}^{1}(\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{V},\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{pre}})

determines a modular form

(1.2.2)

\widehat{\deg}(\widehat{\phi}_{V}(\tau)\cdot\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}^{n-1})=\sum_{m\geq 0}\widehat{\deg}(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}^{\mathrm{tot}}(m)\cdot\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}^{n-1})\cdot q^{m}.

Here
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}^{n-1}
is the
(n-1)
-fold iterated arithmetic intersection.
The peculiar choice of (
1.2.1
) is motivated by the fact
(Theorem
5.5.1
) that it has trivial arithmetic intersection with every irreducible component of the exceptional divisor
\mathrm{Exc}_{V}
, which simplifies the calculation of the coefficients of (
1.2.2
).

Conjecture B
.

If
n>2
, we have the equality of modular forms

\displaystyle\widehat{\deg}(\widehat{\phi}_{V}(\tau)\cdot\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}^{n-1})

(1.2.3)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}-\mathrm{vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V})\sum_{r\mid D}\left(\frac{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}_{V}(0)}{\mathbf{A}_{V}(0)}+\frac{3}{2}\log(D)-\log(r)\right)\gamma_{r}E_{r}(\tau),

in which

E_{r}(\tau)\in M_{n}(\Gamma_{0}(D),\varepsilon^{n})

is the Eisenstein series of Proposition
2.7.1
, and
\gamma_{r}
is the
4^{\mathrm{th}}
root of unity (depending on
V
) defined in Proposition
2.7.3
.

Conjecture
B
is stated in an equivalent form as Conjecture
8.4.1
; the equivalence of the two statements follows from Corollary
8.5.2
.
Because the coefficients of the Eisenstein series
E_{r}(\tau)
are given by explicit formulas,
Conjecture
B
provides us with (conjectural) explicit formulas for the arithmetic intersection multiplicities appearing as the coefficients in (
1.2.2
).
These can be found in §
8.5
, especially (
8.5.3
).

The following is our main evidence for Conjecture
B
.

Theorem C
.

If
n>2
, the modular forms on either side of (
1.2.3
) have the same
m^{\mathrm{th}}
Fourier coefficient for all positive
m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\varepsilon(m)=1
. Moreover, their difference is a cusp form.

The final claim of Theorem
C
allows us to equate the constant terms on both sides of (
1.2.3
), yielding

\widehat{\deg}(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}^{\mathrm{tot}}(0)\cdot\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}^{n-1})=-\mathrm{vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V})\left(\frac{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}_{V}(0)}{\mathbf{A}_{V}(0)}+\frac{3}{2}\log(D)\right).

This equality is equivalent to the second claim in the following theorem, which appears in the text as
Theorems
8.2.3
and
8.4.2
.

Theorem D
.

The arithmetic divisor (
1.2.1
) has complex volume

\operatorname{vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V})=\frac{|\mathrm{CL}({\bm{k}})|}{2^{o(D)-n}|\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}^{\times}|}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{V}(0),

and arithmetic volume

\widehat{\operatorname{vol}}(\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V})=\left(2\frac{\mathbf{A}^{\prime}_{V}(0)}{\mathbf{A}_{V}(0)}+\log(D)\right)\operatorname{vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}).

In practice we do not deduce the second claim of Theorem
D
from the cuspidality claim of Theorem
C
. Instead, the proofs of the two statements are intertwined in a rather complicated way.

In the text, Theorem
A
is deduced from Theorem
D
using the relations between
(
1.1.2
) and (
1.2.1
) found in Theorem
5.5.1
.
This latter theorem also allows us to deduce formulas for the complex and arithmetic volumes of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
from Theorem
D
.
These are found in Theorem
8.2.3
and Corollary
8.5.1
, but the formula for the arithmetic volume is somewhat complicated by correction terms coming from the components of the exceptional divisor
\mathrm{Exc}_{V}\subset\mathcal{S}_{V}
.

We end this section with some remarks about the restriction
\varepsilon(m)=1
in Theorem
C
. In the proof of that theorem, we first prove the equality for all primes
p
satisfying
\varepsilon(p)=1
. The equality for all
m
with
\varepsilon(m)=1
then follows from a result of Bellaïche, found in Appendix
A
.

When
\varepsilon(p)=1
, the strategy for the calculation of the coefficients of (
1.2.2
) is to first relate the Kudla-Rapoport divisor
\mathcal{Z}_{V}(p)
to a Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}_{V^{\flat}}
in one dimension lower, where
V^{\flat}\subset V
is the orthogonal complement to a vector of hermitian norm
p
. This is done in Chapter
6
.
The assumption
\varepsilon(p)=1
guarantees that the hermitian space
V^{\flat}
is relevant (i.e. contains a self-dual lattice), just as
V
was assumed to be, and so by induction one knows the analogue of Theorem
D
with
V
replaced by
V^{\flat}
.
This formula for the arithmetic volume of
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V^{\flat}}
on
\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{V^{\flat}}
then gives a partial formula (Lemma
8.4.3
) for the
p^{\mathrm{th}}
coefficient in (
1.2.2
). Combining this partial formula with the modularity of the generating series
\widehat{\phi}_{V}(\tau)
then proves Theorems
C
and
D
simultaneously.

In contrast, when
\varepsilon(p)=-1
the Kudla-Rapoport divisor
\mathcal{Z}_{V}(p)
should be related to the Shimura variety defined by a hermitian space
V^{\flat}
that is not relevant in the sense above.
Thus carrying out the above inductive procedure when
\varepsilon(p)=-1
would require working with Shimura varieties whose moduli interpretation involves non-principal polarizations, but the results of
[
BHK+20a
]
, and in particular the modularity of
\widehat{\phi}_{V}(\tau)
, are not known in this generality.

In summary, an extension of the modularity results of
[
BHK+20a
]
to Shimura varieties associated to non-relevant hermitian spaces seems to be the missing ingredient needed to remove the restriction
\varepsilon(m)=1
in Theorem
C
.

1.3.
Connections with the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture

Theorem
D
is closely related to a conjecture of Kudla-Rapoport
[
KR14
]
on the degrees of arithmetic
0
-cycles on integral models of unitary Shimura varieties.
Briefly, the Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}_{V}
is endowed not just with the family of Kudla-Rapoport divisors of the previous section, but with special cycles in all codimensions.
In particular, it carries a family of arithmetic
0
-cycles
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}(T)
indexed by
n\times n
hermitian matrices
T
.
The conjecture of Kudla-Rapoport predicts that the arithmetic degrees of these
0
-cycles should agree with the Fourier coefficients of the derivative of an Eisenstein series on the quasi-split unitary group
\operatorname{U}(n,n)
.

For those
0
-cycles with
\det(T)\neq 0
, the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture is now a theorem of Li-Zhang
[
LZ22
]
and He-Li-Shi-Yang
[
HLSY23
]
, who prove the unramified and ramified cases, respectively.
The relevance of Theorem
D
is to the degenerate cases in which
\det(T)=0
, and especially to the most degenerate case
T=0
.
In this case the associated arithmetic
0
-cycle is, up to some correction factors at the primes
p\mid D
, the
n
-fold iterated intersection
of the hermitian line bundle
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
(or
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{V}
) on
\mathcal{S}_{V}
appearing above.
In other words, the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture predicts that the arithmetic volume of
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
is essentially the constant term of the derivative of an Eisenstein series.

We expect that the proof of Theorem
A
makes accessible more degenerate cases of the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture, beyond the most degenerate case
T=0
. For example, consider a matrix of the form

T=\begin{pmatrix}T^{\prime}&\\
&0_{n-d}\end{pmatrix}

with
T^{\prime}
a nonsingular
d\times d
hermitian matrix with
m=\det(T^{\prime})
a squarefree product of primes split in
{\bm{k}}
.
One could hope to relate the arithmetic degree of
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{V}(T)
to the arithmetic intersection number

\widehat{\deg}(\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{tot}}_{V^{\flat}}(m)\cdot\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V^{\flat}}^{n-d})

appearing in (
1.2.2
), where
V^{\flat}
is a hermitian space of signature
(n-d,1)
.
Theorem
C
might then be used to related these arithmetic degrees to degenerate coefficients of a
\mathrm{U}(n,n)
Eisenstein series.

We hope to return to these questions in future work.

1.4.
Outline of the paper

Fix a self-dual
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-lattice
L\subset V
in a
{\bm{k}}
-hermitian space of signature
(n-1,1)
.

We construct in Chapter
2
an Eisenstein series
E_{L}(\tau,s,n)
of weight
n
, valued in a finite dimensional representation of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Its Fourier coefficients can be expressed in terms of local representation densities of the lattice
L
, which we then compute in order to make the coefficients completely explicit.
Evaluating at the non-central point
s_{0}=(n-1)/2
yields a holomorphic Eisenstein series, which we express in terms of the Eisenstein series
E_{r}(\tau)
appearing in Conjecture
B
.

In Chapter
3
we introduce a complex Shimura variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{K}(H,\mathcal{D})
associated to the unitary group
H=\operatorname{U}(V)
. It carries a family of special divisors
Z(m)
indexed by positive
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, and a metrized line bundle
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}
.
To a harmonic Maass form
f
of weight
2-n
we attach a divisor
Z(f)
, defined as a linear combination of
Z(m)
’s.
We then construct a Green function
\Phi(f)
for
Z(f)
using the machinery of regularized theta lifts, and show in Theorem
3.4.2
that the normalized integral

\mathrm{vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}\int_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K}(H,\mathcal{D})}\Phi(f)\operatorname{\mathrm{c}_{1}}(\widehat{\mathcal{L}})^{n-1}

can be expressed in terms of the Fourier coefficients of the derivative of
E_{L}(\tau,s,n)
at
s=s_{0}
.
Here
c_{1}
denotes the first Chern form of a metrized line bundle, as in Remark
4.5.1
.
The proof follows and generalizes ideas of Kudla
[
Kud03
]
, who computed similar integrals on orthogonal Shimura varieties.

In Chapter
4
we work with
{\bm{k}}
-hermitian spaces
W_{0}
and
W
of signatures
(1,0)
and
(n-1,1)
. The Shimura varieties associated to their similitude groups have moduli interpretations, which can be used to construct regular integral models
\mathcal{M}_{W_{0}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{W}
.
We recall the definition of these integral models, their toroidal compactifications, and the structure of their reductions at
p\mid D
. Then we recall those aspects of the Gillet-Soulé arithmetic intersection theory (as extended by Burgos-Kramer-Kühn
[
BGKK07
]
) that are needed in the sequel.

In Chapter
5
we choose
W_{0}
and
W
so that

V\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\bm{k}}(W_{0},W)

as hermitian spaces. This allows us to define the open and closed substack

\mathcal{S}_{V}\subset\mathcal{M}_{W_{0}}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}}\mathcal{M}_{W}

appearing in §
1.2
. It is related to the unitary Shimura variety of Chapter
3
by a finite cover

\mathcal{S}_{V}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathrm{Sh}_{K}(H,\mathcal{D}).

We define the Kudla-Rapoport divisors on
\mathcal{S}_{V}
, and the metrized line bundle
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{V}
.
The universal abelian scheme over
\mathcal{M}_{W}
pulls back to an abelian scheme
A\to\mathcal{S}_{V}
, which determines a metrized Hodge bundle on
\mathcal{S}_{V}
.
The main result of Chapter
5
is Theorem
5.5.1
, which explains the precise connection between
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{V}
and the metrized Hodge bundle.
Going back and forth between these line bundles and (
1.2.1
), which for our purposes is the best behaved of the three, is essential to our methods.

In Chapter
6
we prove Theorems
6.1.2
and
6.1.4
, which are essential to the induction arguments used to prove Theorems
C
and
D
.
The idea is that if
p
is a prime split in
{\bm{k}}
, the Kudla-Rapoport divisor
\mathcal{Z}_{V}(p)
should be closely related to a Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}_{V^{\flat}}
defined in the same way as
\mathcal{S}_{V}
, but with
\mathrm{dim}(V^{\flat})=\mathrm{dim}(V)-1
.
This is true, up to some error terms coming from divisors supported in characteristics dividing
pD
.
Using such a relation, we are able to express the height of the divisor
\mathcal{Z}_{V}(p)
with respect to
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
in terms of the arithmetic volume of the metrized line bundle
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{V^{\flat}}
on the lower-dimensional Shimura variety
\mathcal{S}_{V^{\flat}}
.
Because of the many subtleties in the moduli problem defining the integral models, this is perhaps the most technical part of this work.

In Chapter
7
we recall the modularity results of
[
BHK+20a
]
.
We also prove Theorem
7.3.1
, which can be summarized as saying that the boundary of the toroidal compactification
\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{V}
can, for our purposes, be ignored.

In Chapter
8
, we put everything together to prove the main results.

1.5.
Notation and conventions

Throughout the paper,
{\bm{k}}\subset\mathbb{C}
is a quadratic imaginary field of discriminant
-D
whose associated associated quadratic character is denoted

\varepsilon:(\mathbb{Z}/D\mathbb{Z})^{\times}\to\{\pm 1\}.

The symbol
V
always denotes a
{\bm{k}}
-hermitian space of signature
(n-1,1)
with
n\geq 1
, and the hermitian form is denoted by
\langle-,-\rangle
.
Hermitian forms are always linear in the first variable and conjugate-linear in the second, and are assumed to be nondegenerate.

Beginning in Chapter
4
, and continuing for the rest of the paper, we assume that
D
is odd and the hermitian space admits an
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-lattice
L\subset V
that is
self-dual
, in the sense that

(1.5.1)

L=\{x\in V:\langle x,L\rangle\subset\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}\}.

These restrictions are not imposed in Chapter
2
and Chapter
3
unless stated explicitly.

The term
stack
means separated Deligne-Mumford stack.

1.6.
Acknowledgements

We thank Joel Bellaïche for his help with Appendix
A
,
and Dick Gross for pointing out Remark
1.1.1
.
We also thank the anonymous referee for a careful reading of the manuscript, and for many helpful comments.

Chapter 2
Eisenstein series and theta functions

Fix a
{\bm{k}}
-hermitian space
V
of signature
(n-1,1)
with
n\geq 1
, and denote the hermitian form by
\langle-,-\rangle
.
After reviewing some basics of Eisenstein series, theta functions, and the Siegel-Weil formula,
we attach a particular Eisenstein series to a lattice
L\subset V
, and express its Fourier coefficients in terms of representation densities.
We then compute these representation densities in the case of a self-dual lattice, and so obtain explicit formulas for the Fourier coefficients; these formulas have a different shape depending on whether
n
is even or odd.

2.1.
A seesaw dual reductive pair

Let
V_{0}
be the unique symplectic space over
\mathbb{Q}
of dimension
2
, and denote the symplectic form by
\langle x,y\rangle_{0}
.
Set
V_{0{\bm{k}}}=V_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}{\bm{k}}
, and extend the symplectic form
{\bm{k}}
-linearly in the first argument and
{\bm{k}}
-conjugate-linearly in the second argument. This defines a skew-hermitian form on
V_{0{\bm{k}}}
.

The
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space underlying
V
carries a
\mathbb{Q}
-bilinear form

(2.1.1)

[x,y]=\operatorname{tr}_{{\bm{k}}/\mathbb{Q}}\langle x,y\rangle

of signature
(2n-2,2)
, with associated quadratic form

(2.1.2)

Q(x)=\langle x,x\rangle.

This data determine a seesaw dual reductive pair

\textstyle{G=\operatorname{U}(V_{0{\bm{k}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}=\operatorname{O}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}=\operatorname{Sp}(V_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H=\operatorname{U}(V).}

In particular, there are compatible Weil representations of
G(\mathbb{A})\times H(\mathbb{A})
and
G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})\times H^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
on the space
S(V(\mathbb{A}))
of Schwartz-Bruhat functions on
V(\mathbb{A})
. This will be made explicit in §
2.3
.

Let
e,f
be a basis of
V_{0}
with Gram matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),

and use this to identify

G\cong\left\{g\in\operatorname{Res}_{{\bm{k}}/\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{GL}_{2}:g\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}{}^{t}\bar{g}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\right\}

and
G^{\prime}\cong\operatorname{SL}_{2}
.
Let
P=NM
be the parabolic subgroup of
G
with Levi factor

M=\left\{m(a)=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&\bar{a}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}:a\in\operatorname{Res}_{{\bm{k}}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\right\}

and unipotent radical

N=\left\{n(b)=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}:b\in\mathbb{G}_{a}\right\}.

For a place
p\leq\infty
of
\mathbb{Q}
, let
K_{p}\subset G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be the maximal compact subgroup defined by

K_{p}=\begin{cases}G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cap\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{{\bm{k}},p}),&\text{if $p<\infty$,}\\
G(\mathbb{R})\cap\operatorname{U}(2,\mathbb{R}),&\text{if $p=\infty$,}\end{cases}

and put
K=\prod_{p\leq\infty}K_{p}
.
If we let
{\bm{k}}^{1}
be the torus of norm one elements in
{\bm{k}}^{\times}=\mathrm{Res}_{{\bm{k}}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, there is a natural homomorphism

G^{\prime}\times{\bm{k}}^{1}\xrightarrow{(g,a)\mapsto g\cdot m(a)}G,

and the image of
{\bm{k}}^{1}
is the center of
G
.
This homomorphism induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{SO}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\operatorname{U}(1,\mathbb{R})/\{\pm 1\}\cong K_{\infty}.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the complex upper half-plane.
The first isomorphism in

\mathcal{H}\cong\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})/\operatorname{SO}(2,\mathbb{R})\cong G(\mathbb{R})/K_{\infty}.

sends
\tau=u+iv\in\mathcal{H}
to the element

(2.1.3)

g_{\tau}=n(u)m(v^{1/2})\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

satisfying
g_{\tau}\cdot i=\tau
. The second is induced
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})=G^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\subset G(\mathbb{R})
.

2.2.
Eisenstein series

Given an
s\in\mathbb{C}
and a character
\chi:\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}^{\times}/{\bm{k}}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, let

I(s,\chi)=\operatorname{Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A})}^{G(\mathbb{A})}(\chi|\cdot|_{\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}}^{s})

be the induced representation, realized on the space of smooth
K
-finite functions
\Phi
on
G(\mathbb{A})
satisfying

\Phi(n(b)m(a)g,s)=\chi(a)|a|_{\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\Phi(g,s)

for
b\in\mathbb{A}
and
a\in\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}^{\times}
.
Here
|a|_{\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}}
denotes the norm on
\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}^{\times}
.
In particular, at the archimedian place we have the normalized absolute value
|a|_{\infty}=a\bar{a}
.

Recall that a section of
I(s,\chi)
is called
standard
if its restriction to
K
is independent on
s
.
For a standard section
\Phi
of
I(s,\chi)
, the associated Siegel Eisenstein series

E(g,s,\Phi)=\sum_{\gamma\in P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q})}\Phi(\gamma g,s)

converges absolutely for
\operatorname{Re}(s)>1/2
, and defines an automorphic form on
G(\mathbb{A})
.
The Eisenstein series has meromorphic continuation to all
s\in\mathbb{C}
.
We now describe this Eisenstein series in more classical terms, by restricting it to the subgroup
\operatorname{SL}_{2}\cong G^{\prime}\subset G
.

Assume that
\Phi(s)=\Phi_{\infty}(s)\otimes\Phi_{f}(s)
is a factorizable standard section with
\Phi_{f}=\otimes_{p<\infty}\Phi_{p}(s)
, and that
\Phi_{\infty}
is a normalized standard section of weight
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In other words,
\Phi_{\infty}(1,s)=1
, and for all

k=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SO}(2,\mathbb{R})\subset K_{\infty}

we have
\Phi_{\infty}(gk,s)=\Phi_{\infty}(g,s)\cdot\underline{k}^{\ell}
, where

(2.2.1)

\underline{k}=a+ib\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

Abbreviate
\Gamma=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subset G(\mathbb{A})
, and let
\Gamma_{\infty}=P(\mathbb{Q})\cap\Gamma
be the subgroup of upper triangular matrices.

Lemma 2.2.1
.

If
\Phi_{\infty}
is a normalized standard section of weight
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
as above, then

\displaystyle E(g_{\tau},s,\Phi)

\displaystyle=j(g_{\tau},i)^{-\ell}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}\operatorname{Im}(\gamma\tau)^{s+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot j(\gamma,\tau)^{-\ell}\cdot\Phi_{f}(\gamma).

Here
g_{\tau}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is as in (
2.1.3
), and
j(\gamma,\tau)=c\tau+d
the usual automorphy factor
associated to

\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Proof.

For
\gamma\in\Gamma
we factor

\gamma g_{\tau}=n(\beta)m(\alpha)k

with
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
k\in\operatorname{SO}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then a computation shows that

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{v^{1/2}}{|c\tau+d|},\qquad\underline{k}=\frac{c\bar{\tau}+d}{|c\tau+d|},

and hence

\Phi_{\infty}(\gamma g_{\tau},s)=v^{\ell/2}\cdot\frac{v^{s+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\ell}{2}}}{|c\tau+d|^{2s+1-\ell}}\cdot(c\tau+d)^{-\ell}.

The inclusions
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\subset G(\mathbb{Q})
induce bijections

\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma\cong(P(\mathbb{Q})\cap\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}))\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\cong P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q}),

and hence

\displaystyle E(g_{\tau},s,\Phi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}\Phi_{\infty}(\gamma g_{\tau},s)\Phi_{f}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=v^{\ell/2}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}\operatorname{Im}(\gamma\tau)^{s+\frac{1-\ell}{2}}\cdot(c\tau+d)^{-\ell}\cdot\Phi_{f}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=j(g_{\tau},i)^{-\ell}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}\operatorname{Im}(\gamma\tau)^{s+\frac{1-\ell}{2}}\cdot j(\gamma,\tau)^{-\ell}\cdot\Phi_{f}(\gamma),

as desired.
∎

2.3.
The Siegel-Weil formula

Write
\psi
for the standard additive character of
\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{Q}
, satisfying
\psi_{\infty}(x)=e^{2\pi ix}
.
Let

\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}}:\mathbb{A}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\to\{\pm 1\}

be the idele class character determined by the quadratic extension
{\bm{k}}/\mathbb{Q}
, and fix a character

\chi:\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}^{\times}/{\bm{k}}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}

such that
\chi|_{\mathbb{A}^{\times}}=\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
.

As explained in
[
HKS96
]
and
[
Ich07
]
, the choices of
\psi
and
\chi
determine a Weil representation

\omega=\omega_{\psi,\chi}
of the group
G(\mathbb{A})\times H(\mathbb{A})
on the space of Bruhat-Schwartz functions
S(V(\mathbb{A}))
.
Recalling the
\mathbb{Q}
-bilinear form (
2.1.1
) and associated quadratic form (
2.1.2
), the action is given by the formulas

\displaystyle\omega(m(a))\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\chi(a)|a|_{\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}}^{n/2}\varphi(xa),

\displaystyle\omega(n(b))\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\psi(bQ(x))\varphi(x),

\displaystyle\omega(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix})\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{V(\mathbb{A})}\varphi(y)\psi([x,y])\,dy,

\displaystyle\omega(h)\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\varphi(h^{-1}x)

for
\varphi\in S(V(\mathbb{A}))
and
x\in V(\mathbb{A})
. Here
a\in\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}^{\times}
,
b\in\mathbb{A}
,
h\in H(\mathbb{A})
, and the Fourier transform is taken with respect to the self-dual measure
dy
on
V(\mathbb{A})
.
To a Schwartz function
\varphi
there is an associated theta function

(2.3.1)

\theta(g,h,\varphi)=\sum_{x\in V(\mathbb{Q})}\omega(g,h)\varphi(x).

Abbreviating
s_{0}=(n-1)/2
, there is a
G(\mathbb{A})
-intertwining operator

\displaystyle\lambda:S(V(\mathbb{A}))\to I(s_{0},\chi)

defined by
\lambda(\varphi)(g)=(\omega(g,1)\varphi)(0)
.
We extend
\lambda(\varphi)
to a standard section of
I(s,\chi)
by setting

\lambda(\varphi)(g,s)=|a(g)|_{\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}}^{s-s_{0}}(\omega(g,1)\varphi)(0).

The following theorem is a case of the Siegel-Weil formula; see
[
Wei65
]
or Theorem 1.1 of
[
Ich07
]
.

Theorem 2.3.1
.

Assume that
n>2
, or that
V
is anisotropic. For
\varphi\in S(V(\mathbb{A}))
and
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
we have

E(g,\lambda(\varphi),s_{0})=\kappa\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}\theta(g,h,\varphi)\,dh,

where Haar measure is normalized by
\operatorname{vol}(H(\mathbb{Q})\backslash H(\mathbb{A}))=1
, and

(2.3.2)

\kappa=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }n>1\\
2&\mbox{if }n=1.\end{cases}

2.4.
A special Eisenstein series

We consider Eisenstein series constructed from particular Schwartz functions on
V(\mathbb{A})
.
At the archimedian place we take the Gaussian associated to a majorant and at the non-archimedian places we take the characteristic function of a coset of an
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-lattice.

Let
L\subset V
be an
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-lattice on which the hermitian form is
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-valued.
Its dual lattice under the
\mathbb{Q}
-bilinear form (
2.1.1
) is denoted

(2.4.1)

L^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\{x\in V:[x,L]\subset\mathbb{Z}\}.

For
\mu\in L^{\prime}/L
let

\varphi_{\mu}=\operatorname{char}(\mu+\hat{L})\in S(V(\mathbb{A}_{f}))

be the characteristic function of
\mu+\hat{L}\subset V(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, and denote
by
S_{L}
the subspace of
S(V(\mathbb{A}_{f}))
generated by all
\varphi_{\mu}
with
\mu\in L^{\prime}/L
.
Hence we may identify

S_{L}\cong\mathbb{C}[L^{\prime}/L].

The restriction to
\Gamma=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
of the Weil representation of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
takes the subspace

S_{L}
to itself, giving rise to a representation

(2.4.2)

\displaystyle\omega_{L}:\Gamma\to\operatorname{Aut}(S_{L}).

The corresponding dual representation

\omega_{L}^{\vee}:\Gamma\to\operatorname{Aut}(S_{L}^{\vee})

is given by
\omega_{L}^{\vee}(\gamma)(f)=f\circ\omega_{L}^{-1}(\gamma)
for
f\in S_{L}^{\vee}
.
On the space
S_{L}
we also have the conjugate representation
\bar{\omega}_{L}
defined by

\bar{\omega}_{L}(\gamma)(\varphi)=\overline{\omega_{L}(\gamma)(\bar{\varphi})}

for
\varphi\in S_{L}
.
If we use the standard
\mathbb{C}
-bilinear pairing

(2.4.3)

\Big\langle\sum_{\mu}a_{\mu}\varphi_{\mu},\sum_{\mu}b_{\mu}\varphi_{\mu}\Big\rangle=\sum_{\mu}a_{\mu}b_{\mu}

to identify
S_{L}\cong S_{L}^{\vee}
as
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces, then
\bar{\omega}_{L}=\omega_{L}^{\vee}
,
and this agrees with the representation denoted
\rho_{L}
in
[
Bor98
]
,
[
Bru02
]
, and
[
BY09
]
.

For
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\tau\in\mathcal{H}
, we define an
S_{L}
-valued Eisenstein series

(2.4.4)

\displaystyle E_{L}(\tau,s,\ell)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}\operatorname{Im}(\gamma\tau)^{s+\frac{1-\ell}{2}}(c\tau+d)^{-\ell}\cdot\bar{\omega}_{L}(\gamma)^{-1}\varphi_{0}

of weight
\ell
and representation
\bar{\omega}_{L}
.
Note that this Eisenstein series has a slightly different normalization than the Eisenstein series in equation (2.17) of
[
BY09
]
, since it is adapted to the unitary setting.

Recall the Maass lowering and raising operators in weight
\ell
defined by

L_{\ell}=-2iv^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\tau}},\qquad R_{\ell}=2i\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}+\ell v^{-1}.

They lower (respectively raise) the weight of an automorphic form by
2
.
It is easily seen that

(2.4.5)

\displaystyle L_{\ell}E_{L}(\tau,s,\ell)

\displaystyle=\left(s+\frac{1-\ell}{2}\right)E_{L}(\tau,s,\ell-2)

\displaystyle R_{\ell}E_{L}(\tau,s,\ell)

\displaystyle=\left(s+\frac{1+\ell}{2}\right)E_{L}(\tau,s,\ell+2).

The Eisenstein series
E_{L}(\tau,s,\ell)
is an eigenform of the hyperbolic Laplacian
\Delta_{\ell}
of weight
\ell
(normalized as in (3.1) of
[
BF04
]
) with eigenvalue
(\frac{\ell-1}{2})^{2}-s^{2}
.
The following is a consequence of (
2.4.5
).

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Assume that
n>2
, or that
V
is anisotropic.
The Eisenstein series
E_{L}(\tau,s_{0},n)
of weight
n=\mathrm{dim}(V)
at
s_{0}=(n-1)/2
is holomorphic in
\tau
.
Its derivative

E_{L}^{\prime}(\tau,s_{0},n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}E_{L}^{\prime}(\tau,s,n)\big|_{s=s_{0}}

satisfies

\displaystyle L_{n}(E_{L}^{\prime}(\tau,s_{0},n))

\displaystyle=E_{L}(\tau,s_{0},n-2).

Remark 2.4.2
.

The Eisenstein series
E_{L}(\tau,s,n-2)
is coherent as it arises via the Siegel-Weil formula from the global hermitian space
V
. On the other hand, the Eisenstein series
E_{L}(\tau,s,n)
is incoherent as it is associated to the incoherent collection of local hermitian spaces obtained by replacing
V_{\infty}
by the positive definite hermitian space of dimension
n
and keeping the non-archimedian part. In particular, at
s=0
, the center of symmetry of the functional equation, the Eisenstein series
E_{L}(\tau,s,n)
vanishes identically.

2.5.
Coefficients of Eisenstein series

When the weight of the Eisenstein series (
2.4.4
) is
n=\mathrm{dim}(V)
, we denote its Fourier expansion by

(2.5.1)

\displaystyle E_{L}(\tau,s,n)=\sum_{\mu\in L^{\prime}/L}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}+\langle\mu,\mu\rangle}B(m,\mu;s,v)q^{m}\varphi_{\mu},

where
v=\mathrm{Im}(\tau)
is the imaginary part of
\tau
.
We summarize some well-known facts about the coefficients; details can be found in
[
BK03
]
and
[
KY10
]
.

If
m\neq 0
, there is a factorization

(2.5.2)

\displaystyle B(m,\mu;s,v)=B(m,\mu,s)\cdot\mathcal{W}_{m}(s,v)

in which the non-archimedian contribution
B(m,\mu,s)
is independent of
v
.
The second factor is the archimedian Whittaker function

(2.5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{m}(s,v)=(4\pi|m|v)^{-n/2}e^{2\pi mv}\cdot W_{\operatorname{sgn}(m)\frac{n}{2},s}(4\pi|m|v),

where

W_{\kappa,\mu}(z)=e^{-z/2}z^{1/2+\mu}U(\frac{1}{2}+\mu-\kappa,1+2\mu,z)

denotes the classical confluent hypergeometric function as in
[
AS84
, Chapter 13]
, and

(2.5.4)

\displaystyle U(a,b,z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(a)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}t^{a-1}(1+t)^{b-a-1}\,dt

for
\mathrm{Re}(a)>0
and
|\arg(z)|<\pi/2
.

If
m=0
, the Fourier coefficient has the form

B(0,\mu;s,v)=\delta_{\mu,0}\cdot v^{s+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{2}}+B(0,\mu,s)\cdot v^{-s+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n}{2}},

where
B(0,\mu,s)
is independent of
v
, and
\delta_{\mu,0}
is the Kronecker delta.

Remark 2.5.1
.

Note that our normalization of the archimedian Whittaker function in (
2.5.3
) differs from the normalization in
[
KY10
, Proposition 2.3]
. The normalizing factor
\Gamma(s+\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{2})^{-1}
for
m>0
, and
\Gamma(s-\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{2})^{-1}
for
m<0
, appears in loc. cit. In our case this factor is included in the normalization of the
B(m,\mu,s)
, since this leads to slightly cleaner formulas in Theorem
3.4.2
.

The integral representation (
2.5.4
) can be used to
describe the asymptotic behavior of the Whittaker function as
v\to\infty
, which is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{m}(s,v)=\begin{cases}1+O(v^{-1})&\mbox{if }m>0\\
O(e^{-4\pi|m|v})&\mbox{if }m<0,\end{cases}

locally uniformly in
s
and
m
.

We will also require the following lemma on the value at
s_{0}=(n-1)/2
of the Whittaker function and its derivative
\mathcal{W}_{m}^{\prime}(s_{0},v)=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathcal{W}_{m}(s,v)\mid_{s=s_{0}}
.

Lemma 2.5.2
.

The special value at
s_{0}
of the Whittaker function is

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{m}(s_{0},v)=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }m>0\\
\Gamma(1-n,4\pi|m|v)&\mbox{if }m<0.\end{cases}

For
m>0
we have

\mathcal{W}_{m}^{\prime}(s_{0},v)=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{j}\frac{\Gamma(j)}{(4\pi mv)^{j}}.

In particular,

\mathcal{W}_{m}^{\prime}(s_{0},v)=O(v^{-1}),\quad\text{for $v\to\infty$.}

Proof.

We only prove this for
m>0
and leave the similar case when
m<0
to the reader.
According to (
2.5.3
) and (
2.5.4
), we have for
m>0
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{m}(s,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\pi mv)^{s-s_{0}}}{\Gamma(s-s_{0})}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-4\pi mvt}t^{s-s_{0}-1}(1+t)^{s+s_{0}}\,dt

\displaystyle=1+\frac{(4\pi mv)^{s-s_{0}}}{\Gamma(s-s_{0})}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-4\pi mvt}t^{s-s_{0}-1}((1+t)^{s+s_{0}}-1)\,dt,

where the latter integral converges absolutely for
\mathrm{Re}(s)>s_{0}-1
. We immediately see that
\mathcal{W}_{m}(s_{0},v)=1
, and in addition that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{m}(s_{0},v)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-4\pi mvt}((1+t)^{n-1}-1)\,\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-4\pi mvt}t^{j}\,\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{j}\frac{\Gamma(j)}{(4\pi mv)^{j}}.

This gives the claimed formula and the bound for
v\to\infty
.
∎

We now turn to the explicit calculation of the factor
B(m,\mu,s)
in (
2.5.2
), using formulas from
[
BK03
]
and
[
KY10
]
.
Fix
\mu\in L^{\prime}/L
, and let

d_{\mu}=\min\{b\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}:b\mu=0\}

be the order of
\mu
.
For
m\in\mathbb{Z}+Q(\mu)
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
N_{m,\mu}(a)
the modulo
a
representation number

\displaystyle N_{m,\mu}(a)=\#\{r\in L/aL:\;Q(r+\mu)\equiv m\pmod{a\mathbb{Z}}\}.

For a prime
p
, set

(2.5.5)

\displaystyle L^{(p)}_{m,\mu}(p^{-s})=(1-p^{-s+2n-1})\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}N_{m,\mu}(p^{\nu})p^{-\nu s}

so that

\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}N_{m,\mu}(a)a^{-s}=\zeta(s-2n+1)\prod_{p}L^{(p)}_{m,\mu}(p^{-s}).

Proposition 2.5.3
.

For nonzero
m\in\mathbb{Z}+Q(\mu)
we have

B(m,\mu,s)=-\frac{2^{n}\pi^{s+\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{|L^{\prime}/L|}}\cdot\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{|m|^{s+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(s+\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{2})}\prod_{p}L^{(p)}_{m,\mu}(p^{-2s-n})&\mbox{if }m>0\\[12.91663pt]
\displaystyle\frac{|m|^{s+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(s-\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{2})}\prod_{p}L^{(p)}_{m,\mu}(p^{-2s-n})&\mbox{if }m<0.\end{cases}

Proof.

This is obtained from Proposition 3.2 of
[
BK03
]
by noticing that our Eisenstein series
E_{L}(\tau,s,n)
is
1/2
the Eisenstein series
E_{0}(\tau,s-\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{2})
of
[
BK03
]
with
\kappa=n
and
r=2n
.
∎

According to equation (3.20) of
[
BK03
]
, the Euler factors
L^{(p)}_{m,\mu}(p^{-s})
are polynomials in
p^{-s}
.
Moreover, by (3.23) of
[
BK03
]
, if
p
does not divide
d_{\mu}^{2}m|L^{\prime}/L|
then

L^{(p)}_{m,\mu}(p^{-s})=1-\chi_{F}(p)p^{n-1-s},

where
\chi_{F}
is the quadratic Dirichlet character associated to the quadratic extension
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{(-1)^{n}|L^{\prime}/L|})
.
This implies that the Euler product in Proposition
2.5.3
converges absolutely for
\operatorname{Re}(s)>0
and that
B(m,\mu,s_{0})
is rational if
n>1
(so that
s_{0}=\frac{n-1}{2}
lies in the region of convergence).
In this case it is nonpositive for positive
m
and vanishes for negative
m
.

Later we will require the following lemma on the behavior of the coefficients of the Eisenstein series in the limit
v\to\infty
.

Lemma 2.5.4
.

Assume that
n>2
, or that
V
is anisotropic.
The coefficients
B^{\prime}(m,\mu;s_{0},v)
of
E_{L}^{\prime}(\tau,s_{0},n)
satisfy

\lim_{v\to\infty}\big(B^{\prime}(m,\mu;s_{0},v)-\kappa\delta_{m,0}\delta_{\mu,0}\log v\big)=\begin{cases}B^{\prime}(m,\mu,s_{0})&\text{if $m>0$}\\
B^{\prime}(0,\mu,s_{0})&\text{if $m=0$ and $n=1$}\\
0&\text{if $m=0$ and $n>1$}\\
0&\text{if $m<0$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

For
m\neq 0
the assertion follows from Proposition
2.5.3
together with Lemma
2.5.2
on the asymptotic behavior of the Whittaker function
\mathcal{W}_{m}(s,v)
and its derivative at
s=s_{0}
.

For
m=0
, we consider the Laurent expansion of
B(0,\mu;s,v)
at
s=s_{0}
, which is given by

\displaystyle B(0,\mu;s,v)=\delta_{\mu,0}+B(0,\mu,s_{0})v^{-2s_{0}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}{}+\left(\delta_{\mu,0}\log(v)-B(0,\mu,s_{0})\log(v)v^{-2s_{0}}+B^{\prime}(0,\mu,s_{0})v^{-2s_{0}}\right)(s-s_{0})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}{}+O((s-s_{0})^{2}).

If
n>1
, then the holomorphicity of
E_{L}(\tau,s_{0},n)
implies that
B(0,\mu,s_{0})=0
. Since
2s_{0}>0
, the term involving
v^{-2s_{0}}
vanishes in the limit
v\to\infty
, and we obtain the assertion.

If
n=1
then
s_{0}=0
is the center of symmetry of the functional equation of the incoherent Eisenstein series, and hence
E_{L}(\tau,0,n)
vanishes. The vanishing of the constant Fourier coefficient implies

B(0,\mu,0)=-\delta_{\mu,0},

and consequently

B^{\prime}(0,\mu;0,v)=2\delta_{\mu,0}\log(v)+B^{\prime}(0,\mu,s_{0}).

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

2.6.
Self-dual lattices

In this section we assume the
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-lattice
L\subset V
is self-dual (
1.5.1
) under the hermitian form, and that
D=-\mathrm{disc}({\bm{k}})
is odd.
The first of these assumptions implies that (
2.4.1
) satisfies
L^{\prime}=\mathfrak{d}_{\bm{k}}^{-1}L
, where
\mathfrak{d}_{\bm{k}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
is the different.
We will determine the coefficient
B(m,\mu,s)
from (
2.5.2
) more explicitly.

To compute the Euler factors
L^{(p)}_{m,\mu}(p^{-s})
in Proposition
2.5.3
, we need to determine the representation numbers
N_{m,\mu}(p^{\nu})
.
We now derive explicit formulas for these quantities using finite Fourier transforms and formulas for certain lattice Gauss sums. As we will only compute the coefficients for
\mu=0
, we abbreviate

N_{m}(p^{\nu})=N_{m,0}(p^{\nu})
.

Fix an
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. For
c\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, consider the Gauss sum

G(a,c)=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}/c\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}}e\left(\frac{a\operatorname{N}(x)}{c}\right).

We are mainly interested in the case when
c=p^{\nu}
is a prime power.

Lemma 2.6.1
.

Let
p^{\nu}
be a prime power.
If we write

\gcd(a,p^{\nu})=p^{\alpha}
with
0\leq\alpha\leq\nu
and
a=p^{\alpha}a^{\prime}
with
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

G(a,p^{\nu})=\begin{cases}p^{2\nu}&\text{if $\alpha=\nu$}\\
p^{\alpha+\nu}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{\nu-\alpha}&\text{if $\alpha<\nu$ and $p\nmid D$}\\
\epsilon_{p}p^{\alpha+\nu+\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{a^{\prime}}{p}\right)\left(\frac{D^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{\nu-\alpha-1}&\text{if $\alpha<\nu$ and $p\mid D$.}\end{cases}

Here, in the final case, we have set
D^{\prime}=D/p
and

\epsilon_{r}=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }r\equiv 1\pmod{4}\\
i&\mbox{if }r\equiv 3\pmod{4}.\end{cases}

for any odd positive integer
r
.

Proof.

It is easily seen that

G(a,p^{\nu})=p^{2\alpha}G(a^{\prime},p^{\nu-\alpha}).

Hence we may assume that
a
is coprime to
p
.
Now, if
p
is odd, the assertion can be easily deduced from the classical Gauss sum

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}/c\mathbb{Z}}e\left(\frac{ax^{2}}{c}\right)=\epsilon_{c}\sqrt{c}\left(\frac{a}{c}\right)

for odd positive
c
with
\gcd(c,a)=1
.

For
p=2
and
a
odd we have

(2.6.1)

\displaystyle G(a,2^{\nu})=\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}/2^{\nu}\mathbb{Z}}e\left(a\frac{x^{2}+xy+\frac{1+D}{4}y^{2}}{2^{\nu}}\right),

where we have used that
D
is odd.
If
\nu=1
the asserted formula is easily checked. Hence, assume that
\nu\geq 2
. Then we use the classical Gauss sum

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}/2^{\nu}\mathbb{Z}}e\left(a\frac{x^{2}+xy}{2^{\nu}}\right)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle 0,&2\nmid y,\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle e\left(-a\frac{y^{2}}{2^{\nu+2}}\right)\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}/2^{\nu}\mathbb{Z}}e\left(a\frac{x^{2}}{2^{\nu}}\right),&2\mid y,\end{cases}

to rewrite (
2.6.1
) as follows:

\displaystyle G(a,2^{\nu})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}/2^{\nu}\mathbb{Z}}e\left(a\frac{x^{2}+Dy^{2}}{2^{\nu}}\right).

Inserting the evaluation of the Gauss sum

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}/2^{\nu}\mathbb{Z}}e\left(a\frac{x^{2}}{2^{\nu}}\right)=(1+i)\epsilon_{a}^{-1}2^{\nu/2}\left(\frac{2^{\nu}}{a}\right),

we finally find that

\displaystyle G(a,2^{\nu})=2^{\nu}\left(\frac{2^{\nu}}{D}\right)=2^{\nu}\left(\frac{-D}{2}\right)^{\nu},

concluding the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 2.6.2
.

Let
p^{\nu}
be a prime power.
If we write

\gcd(a,p^{\nu})=p^{\alpha}
with
0\leq\alpha\leq\nu
and
a=p^{\alpha}a^{\prime}
with
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
the Gauss sum

\displaystyle G_{L}(a,p^{\nu})=\sum_{x\in L/p^{\nu}L}e\left(\frac{a\langle x,x\rangle}{p^{\nu}}\right)

is given by

G_{L}(a,p^{\nu})=\begin{cases}p^{2n\nu}&\text{if $\alpha=\nu$ }\\
p^{n\alpha+n\nu}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{n(\nu-\alpha)}&\text{if $\alpha<\nu$ and $p\nmid D$}\\
\epsilon_{p}^{n}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{n\alpha+n\nu+\frac{n}{2}}\left(\frac{a^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{n}\left(\frac{D^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{n(\nu-\alpha-1)}&\text{if $\alpha<\nu$ and $p\mid D$,}\end{cases}

where
\epsilon_{p}
and
D^{\prime}
have the same meaning as in Lemma
2.6.1
.

Proof.

The Gauss sum
G_{L}(a,p^{\nu})
only depends on
L_{p}=L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
If we let
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
be an orthogonal
\mathcal{O}_{{\bm{k}},p}
-module basis of
L_{p}
(which exists because of our hypothesis that
D
is odd), then

G_{L}(a,p^{\nu})=G(a\langle b_{1},b_{1}\rangle,p^{\nu})\cdots G(a\langle b_{n},b_{n}\rangle,p^{\nu}),

and the claim follows from Lemma
2.6.1
.
Note that in the case
p\mid D
, standard formulas for the Hilbert symbol imply

\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)=\left(\frac{\langle b_{1},b_{1}\rangle}{p}\right)\cdots\left(\frac{\langle b_{n},b_{n}\rangle}{p}\right).\qed

Proposition 2.6.3
.

Assume that
n
is even, and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
is nonzero.
Fix a prime
p
, and factor
m=p^{\beta}m^{\prime}
with
\gcd(m^{\prime},p)=1
.
If
p\nmid D
then

L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-s})=(1-p^{n-s-1})\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\beta}p^{(n-s)\gamma}.

If
p\mid D
then

\displaystyle L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-s})

\displaystyle=1-\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{\frac{n}{2}}p^{n-1-s}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{\frac{n}{2}}\left(1-p^{n-1-s}\right)\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\beta}p^{(n-s)\gamma}.

Proof.

We compute the representation number
N_{m}(p^{\nu})
using the identity

\displaystyle N_{m}(p^{\nu})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{\nu}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}/p^{\nu}\mathbb{Z}}G_{L}(a,p^{\nu})e\Big(-\frac{am}{p^{\nu}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{\nu}}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu}\sum_{a^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/p^{\nu-\alpha}\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}G_{L}(p^{\alpha}a^{\prime},p^{\nu})e\Big(-\frac{a^{\prime}m}{p^{\nu-\alpha}}\Big).

As
n
is even, Lemma
2.6.2
implies

G_{L}(a,p^{\nu})=\begin{cases}p^{2n\nu}&\text{if $\alpha=\nu$}\\
p^{n\alpha+n\nu}&\text{if $\alpha<\nu$ and $p\nmid D$ }\\
\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{n/2}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{n\alpha+n\nu+\frac{n}{2}}&\text{if $\alpha<\nu$ and $p\mid D$.}\end{cases}

If
p\nmid D
we obtain

\displaystyle N_{m}(p^{\nu})

\displaystyle=p^{n\nu-\nu}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu}p^{n\alpha}\sum_{a^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/p^{\nu-\alpha}\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}e\Big(-\frac{a^{\prime}m}{p^{\nu-\alpha}}\Big)

\displaystyle=p^{n\nu-\nu}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu}p^{n\alpha}\sum_{d\mid\gcd(p^{\nu-\alpha},m)}\mu\Big(\frac{p^{\nu-\alpha}}{d}\Big)d

\displaystyle=p^{n\nu-\nu}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu}p^{n\alpha}\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\min(\nu-\alpha,\beta)}\mu\big(p^{\nu-\alpha-\gamma}\big)p^{\gamma}.

Here we have used the evaluation of the Ramanujan sum.
Consequently, the Euler factor (
2.5.5
) is

\displaystyle L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-s})

\displaystyle=(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}N_{m}(p^{\nu})p^{-\nu s}

\displaystyle=(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\beta}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu=\alpha+\gamma}^{\infty}p^{n\alpha+\gamma}\mu(p^{\nu-\alpha-\gamma})p^{(n-1-s)\nu}

\displaystyle=(1-p^{n-s-1})\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\beta}p^{(n-s)\gamma}.

In the case
p\mid D
we find

\displaystyle N_{m}(p^{\nu})

\displaystyle=p^{2n\nu-\nu}+\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{n/2}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{n\nu-\nu+\frac{n}{2}}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu-1}p^{n\alpha}\sum_{a^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/p^{\nu-\alpha}\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}e\Big(-\frac{a^{\prime}m}{p^{\nu-\alpha}}\Big)

\displaystyle=p^{2n\nu-\nu}+\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{n/2}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{n\nu-\nu+\frac{n}{2}}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu-1}p^{n\alpha}\sum_{d\mid\gcd(p^{\nu-\alpha},m)}\mu\Big(\frac{p^{\nu-\alpha}}{d}\Big)d

\displaystyle=p^{2n\nu-\nu}+\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{n/2}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{n\nu-\nu+\frac{n}{2}}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu-1}p^{n\alpha}\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\min(\nu-\alpha,\beta)}\mu\big(p^{\nu-\alpha-\gamma}\big)p^{\gamma}.

Consequently, the Euler factor
L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-s})
of (
2.5.5
) is

\displaystyle(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}N_{m}(p^{\nu})p^{-\nu s}

\displaystyle=1+\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{\frac{n}{2}}(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}p^{(n-1-s)\nu}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu-1}\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\min(\nu-\alpha,\beta)}p^{n\alpha+\gamma}\mu(p^{\nu-\alpha-\gamma})

\displaystyle=1+\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{\frac{n}{2}}(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\beta}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\geq\alpha+\gamma\\
\nu\geq\alpha+1\end{subarray}}p^{(n-1-s)\nu}p^{n\alpha+\gamma}\mu(p^{\nu-\alpha-\gamma}).

The contribution of
\gamma=0
to the latter sum is given by

\displaystyle(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\geq\alpha+1\end{subarray}}p^{(n-1-s)\nu}p^{n\alpha}\mu(p^{\nu-\alpha})=-p^{n-1-s}.

The contribution of
\gamma\geq 1
is equal to

\displaystyle(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\beta}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\geq\alpha+\gamma\end{subarray}}p^{n\alpha+\gamma}p^{(n-1-s)\nu}\mu(p^{\nu-\alpha-\gamma})

\displaystyle=(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\beta}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}p^{n\alpha+\gamma}\left(p^{(n-1-s)(\alpha+\gamma)}-p^{(n-1-s)(\alpha+\gamma+1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1-p^{n-1-s}\right)\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\beta}p^{(n-s)\gamma}.

Putting the terms together, we find

\displaystyle L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-s})

\displaystyle=1-\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{\frac{n}{2}}p^{n-1-s}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{\frac{n}{2}}\left(1-p^{n-1-s}\right)\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\beta}p^{(n-s)\gamma}.

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Proposition 2.6.4
.

Assume
n
is odd, and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
is nonzero.
Fix a prime
p
, and factor
m=p^{\beta}m^{\prime}
with
\gcd(m^{\prime},p)=1
.
If
p\nmid D
then

L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-s})=\left(1-\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)p^{n-s-1}\right)\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\beta}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{\gamma}p^{(n-s)\gamma}.

If
p\mid D
then

L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-s})=1+\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\left(\frac{D^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{\beta}\left(\frac{m^{\prime}}{p}\right)p^{(\beta+3/2)n-1/2-(\beta+1)s},

where we have set
D^{\prime}=D/p
.

Proof.

We argue in the same way as in the proof of Proposition
2.6.3
, using the identity

\displaystyle N_{m}(p^{\nu})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{\nu}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}/p^{\nu}\mathbb{Z}}G_{L}(a,p^{\nu})e\Big(-\frac{am}{p^{\nu}}\Big)

and the value of the Gauss sum
G_{L}(a,p^{\nu})
computed in Lemma
2.6.2
.
If we write
a=p^{\alpha}a^{\prime}
with
a^{\prime}
coprime to
p
, we have

G_{L}(a,p^{\nu})=\begin{cases}p^{2n\nu}&\text{if $\alpha=\nu$ }\\
p^{n\alpha+n\nu}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{\nu-\alpha}&\text{if $\alpha<\nu$ and $p\nmid D$ }\\
\epsilon_{p}^{n}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{n\alpha+n\nu+\frac{n}{2}}\left(\frac{a^{\prime}}{p}\right)\left(\frac{D^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{\nu-\alpha-1}&\text{if $\alpha<\nu$ and $p\mid D$.}\end{cases}

If
p\nmid D
we obtain

\displaystyle N_{m}(p^{\nu})

\displaystyle=p^{n\nu-\nu}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu}p^{n\alpha}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{\nu-\alpha}\sum_{a^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/p^{\nu-\alpha}\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}e\Big(-\frac{a^{\prime}m}{p^{\nu-\alpha}}\Big)

\displaystyle=p^{n\nu-\nu}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu}p^{n\alpha}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{\nu-\alpha}\sum_{d\mid\gcd(p^{\nu-\alpha},m)}\mu\Big(\frac{p^{\nu-\alpha}}{d}\Big)d

\displaystyle=p^{n\nu-\nu}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu}p^{n\alpha}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{\nu-\alpha}\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\min(\nu-\alpha,\beta)}\mu\big(p^{\nu-\alpha-\gamma}\big)p^{\gamma}.

Here we have used the evaluation of the Ramanujan sum.
Hence, the Euler factor (
2.5.5
) is

\displaystyle L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-s})

\displaystyle=(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}N_{m}(p^{\nu})p^{-\nu s}

\displaystyle=(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\beta}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu=\alpha+\gamma}^{\infty}p^{n\alpha+\gamma}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{\nu-\alpha}\mu(p^{\nu-\alpha-\gamma})p^{(n-1-s)\nu}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)p^{n-s-1}\right)\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\beta}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{\gamma}p^{(n-s)\gamma}.

When
p\mid D
we find

\displaystyle N_{m}(p^{\nu})=p^{2n\nu-\nu}

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{p}^{n}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{n\nu-\nu+\frac{n}{2}}

\displaystyle\phantom{+}{}\times\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\nu-1}\left(\frac{D^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{\nu-\alpha-1}p^{n\alpha}\sum_{a^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/p^{\nu-\alpha}\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}\left(\frac{a^{\prime}}{p}\right)e\Big(-\frac{a^{\prime}m}{p^{\nu-\alpha}}\Big).

The latter Gauss sum is equal to
p^{\nu-\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon_{p}\left(\frac{-m^{\prime}}{p}\right)
if
\beta=\nu-\alpha-1
and is zero otherwise. Inserting this we find that
N_{m}(p^{\nu})
is equal to

\displaystyle p^{2n\nu-\nu}

\displaystyle\cdot\begin{cases}1+\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}\left(\frac{D^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{\beta}\left(\frac{-m^{\prime}}{p}\right)p^{(1-n)(\beta+1/2)}&\text{if $\nu>\beta$}\\[4.30554pt]
1&\text{if $\nu\leq\beta$,}\end{cases}

and hence
L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-s})
is equal to

\displaystyle(1-p^{2n-s-1})\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}N_{m}(p^{\nu})p^{-\nu s}

\displaystyle=1+\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}\left(\frac{D^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{\beta}\left(\frac{-m^{\prime}}{p}\right)p^{(1-n)(\beta+1/2)}p^{(2n-1-s)(\beta+1)}

\displaystyle=1+\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\left(\frac{D^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{\beta}\left(\frac{m^{\prime}}{p}\right)p^{(\beta+3/2)n-1/2-(\beta+1)s}.

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Recall the function
\mathbf{a}_{n}(s)
of (
1.1.4
), and abbreviate
s_{0}=(n-1)/2
.
We now express the coefficients
B(m,0,s)
in terms of

\mathbf{a}_{n}(s-s_{0})=\frac{D^{n/2}\Gamma\left(s+\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)L\left(2s+1,\varepsilon^{n}\right)}{2^{n}\pi^{s+\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{2}}}.

Corollary 2.6.5
.

Assume that
n\geq 2
is even.
If
m>0
then

B(m,0,s)=-\frac{m^{s+s_{0}}}{\mathbf{a}_{n}(s-s_{0})}\cdot\prod_{p\nmid D}\sum_{\gamma=0}^{v_{p}(m)}p^{-2s\gamma}\cdot\prod_{p\mid D}\frac{L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-2s-n})}{1-p^{-2s-1}}.

If
m>0
is prime to
D
then

B(m,0,s)=-\frac{m^{s+s_{0}}\sigma_{-2s}(m)}{\mathbf{a}_{n}(s-s_{0})}\cdot\prod_{p\mid D}\frac{1-\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{\frac{n}{2}-1-2s}}{1-p^{-2s-1}},

where
\sigma_{s}(m)=\sum_{d\mid m}d^{s}
is the usual divisor sum.

Proof.

Combine Propositions
2.5.3
and
2.6.3
.
∎

Corollary 2.6.6
.

Assume that
n\geq 1
is odd.
If
m>0
then

\displaystyle B(m,0,s)=-\frac{m^{s+s_{0}}}{\mathbf{a}_{n}(s-s_{0})}\cdot\prod_{p\nmid D}\sum_{\gamma=0}^{v_{p}(m)}\varepsilon(p)^{\gamma}p^{-2s\gamma}\cdot\prod_{p\mid D}L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-2s-n}).

If
m>0
is prime to
D
, then

\displaystyle B(m,0,s)=-\frac{m^{s+s_{0}}\sigma_{-2s,\varepsilon}(m)}{\mathbf{a}_{n}(s-s_{0})}\cdot\prod_{p\mid D}\left(1+\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\!\left(\frac{m}{p}\right)\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)p^{\frac{n-1}{2}-2s}\right),

where
\sigma_{s,\varepsilon}(m)=\sum_{d\mid m}\varepsilon(d)d^{s}
is the usual divisor sum.

Proof.

Combine Propositions
2.5.3
and
2.6.4
.
∎

Remark 2.6.7
.

If
n=1
, then

s_{0}=0
is the center of symmetry of the functional equation of the incoherent Eisenstein series
E_{L}(\tau,s,1)
. Hence
E_{L}(\tau,s,1)
vanishes identically (to odd order) at
s=0
. For all positive
m
this implies that the coefficients
B(m,\mu,s_{0})
also vanish. Consequently, in view of
Proposition
2.5.3
, there must be at least one prime
p<\infty
for which the local factor
L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-1})
is zero. According to Proposition
2.6.4
, this happens exactly when

\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)=-\begin{cases}\left(\frac{-D}{p}\right)^{\beta},&\text{if $p\nmid D$,}\\[4.30554pt]
\left(\frac{D^{\prime}}{p}\right)^{\beta}\left(\frac{m^{\prime}}{p}\right),&\text{if $p\mid D$,}\end{cases}

where we have used the notation of the cited proposition. In terms of the local Hilbert symbol this is equivalent to the condition
\operatorname{inv}_{p}(V)=-(-D,m)_{p}
, which means that
V_{p}
does not represent
m
.

The derivatives
B^{\prime}(m,0,s_{0})
of the coefficients can be computed as follows.
Let
\mathbb{V}
be the incoherent hermitian space over
\mathbb{A}_{\bm{k}}
whose non-archimedian contribution is given by
V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{A}_{f}
and whose archimedian contribution is
V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
with the positive definite hermitian form
-\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Following
[
Kud97
]
we define the ‘Diff set’ associated with
\mathbb{V}
and
m\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
by

\operatorname{Diff}(\mathbb{V},m)=\{p\leq\infty\mid\;\text{$\mathbb{V}_{p}$ does not represent $m$}\}.

This is a finite set of places of
\mathbb{Q}
of odd cardinality. When
m>0
it only consists of finite primes. The above argument shows that
L^{(p)}_{m}(p^{-1})=0
if and only if
p\in\operatorname{Diff}(\mathbb{V},m)
. Hence
B^{\prime}(m,0,s_{0})\neq 0
if and only if
\operatorname{Diff}(\mathbb{V},m)
consists of a single prime
q
. By Proposition
2.5.3
we find for
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}

in this case that

B^{\prime}(m,0,0)=-\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{D}}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$p$ prime}\\
p\neq q\end{subarray}}L_{m}^{(p)}(p^{-1})\cdot\frac{d}{ds}L_{m}^{(q)}(q^{-2s-1})\big|_{s=0}.

This expression can be evaluated explicitly by means of Proposition
2.6.4
. For instance, when
(m,D)=1
and
m=q^{\beta}m^{\prime}
with
m^{\prime}
coprime to
q
, we see that
\operatorname{inv}_{q}(V)=1
, the prime
q
must be inert in
{\bm{k}}
, and
\beta
must be odd. Under these conditions we find that

B^{\prime}(m,0,0)=-\frac{2^{o(D)+1}\pi\,\sigma_{0,\varepsilon}(m^{\prime})}{\sqrt{D}L(\varepsilon,1)}\left(\beta+1\right)\cdot\log(q),

where
o(D)
denotes the number of prime factors of
D
.

Regardless of whether
n
is even or odd, we have the following lower bound for the coefficients of the Eisenstein series
E_{L}(\tau,s_{0},n)
.

Corollary 2.6.8
.

Assume that
n\geq 2
.
There is a constant
C>0
, depending only on
L
, such that

-B(m,0,s_{0})>C\cdot m^{n-1}

for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

Proof.

This follows easily from Corollary
2.6.5
and Corollary
2.6.6
.
∎

2.7.
Decomposing the Eisenstein series

As in the previous section, we assume the
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-lattice
L\subset V
is self-dual under the hermitian form, and that
D=-\mathrm{disc}({\bm{k}})
is odd. We also assume that
V
has dimension
n\geq 2
.

Consider again the holomorphic Eisenstein series

E_{L}(\tau,s_{0},n)=\sum_{m\geq 0}\sum_{\mu\in L^{\prime}/L}B(m,\mu,s_{0})q^{m}\varphi_{\mu}

valued in
S_{L}=\mathbb{C}[L^{\prime}/L]
obtained by evaluating (
2.5.1
) at
s_{0}=(n-1)/2
.
Applying the linear functional
S_{L}\to\mathbb{C}
sending
\varphi_{0}\mapsto 1
and
\varphi_{\mu}\mapsto 0
for
\mu\neq 0
yields an Eisenstein series

\sum_{m\geq 0}B(m,0,s_{0})\cdot q^{m}\in M_{n}(\Gamma_{0}(D),\varepsilon^{n}),

which can be decomposed as a linear combination of classical Eisenstein series indexed by the cusps

\infty_{r}=\frac{r}{D}\in\Gamma_{0}(D)\backslash\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{Q})

as
r\mid D
varies. In this section we make this decomposition explicit.

For any divisor
r\mid D
set
r^{\prime}=D/r
. Our assumption that
D
is odd implies that the quadratic character
\varepsilon:(\mathbb{Z}/D\mathbb{Z})^{\times}\to\{\pm 1\}
determined by
{\bm{k}}
is

\varepsilon(a)=\left(\frac{a}{D}\right).

Hence we may factor
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{r}\cdot\varepsilon_{r^{\prime}}
with

\varepsilon_{r}(a)=\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\varepsilon_{r^{\prime}}(a)=\left(\frac{a}{r^{\prime}}\right).

Define the quadratic Gauss sum

\tau(\varepsilon_{r})=\sum_{a\in(\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z})^{\times}}\varepsilon_{r}(a)e^{2\pi ia/r}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{r}&\mbox{if }r\equiv 1\pmod{4}\\
i\sqrt{r}&\mbox{if }r\equiv 3\pmod{4},\end{cases}

and similarly with
r
replaced by
r^{\prime}
.

Proposition 2.7.1
.

For every divisor
r\mid D
there is an Eisenstein series

E_{r}(\tau)=\sum_{m\geq 0}e_{r}(m)\cdot q^{m}\in M_{n}(\Gamma_{0}(D),\varepsilon^{n})

whose Fourier coefficients are as follows.
The constant term is

e_{r}(0)=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }r=1\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
n
is even the coefficients indexed by
m>0
are

e_{r}(m)=\frac{r^{n/2}(-2\pi i)^{n}}{D^{n}\Gamma(n)L_{D}(n,\varepsilon^{n})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\mid m\\
c>0\\
\gcd(m/c,r)=1\end{subarray}}c^{n-1}\sum_{d\mid\gcd(c,r^{\prime})}d\mu(r^{\prime}/d).

If
n
is odd the coefficients indexed by
m>0
are

e_{r}(m)=\varepsilon_{r}(r^{\prime})\frac{r^{n/2}(-2\pi i)^{n}\tau(\varepsilon_{r^{\prime}})}{D^{n}\Gamma(n)L_{D}(n,\varepsilon^{n})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\mid m\\
c>0\\
\gcd(m/c,r)=1\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{r}(m/c)\varepsilon_{r^{\prime}}(c)\cdot c^{n-1}.

In both formulas
L_{D}(s,\varepsilon^{n})
is the Dirichlet
L
-function with Euler factors at all
\ell\mid D
removed.

Proof.

For each
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}/D\mathbb{Z}
define an Eisenstein series

G_{(s,t)}(z)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c,d\in\mathbb{Z}\\
(c,d)\neq(0,0)\\
(c,d)\equiv(s,t)\pmod{D}\end{subarray}}(cz+d)^{-n}.

Theorem 7.1.3 of
[
Miy06
]
implies that

E_{r}(z)=\frac{\varepsilon^{n}_{r}(r^{\prime})}{2r^{n/2}L_{D}(n,\varepsilon^{n})}\sum_{s,t\in\mathbb{Z}/D\mathbb{Z}}\varepsilon_{r}^{n}(s)\varepsilon_{r^{\prime}}^{n}(t)G_{(s,t)}(r^{\prime}z)

has the desired Fourier expansion.
∎

Remark 2.7.2
.

The Eisenstein series of Proposition
2.7.1
agree with the Eisenstein series denoted the same way in §4.4 of
[
BHK+20a
]
. In particular,
E_{r}
is nonvanishing at the cusp
\infty_{r}=r/D
, and vanishes at all other cusps.

Proposition 2.7.3
.

We have the equality

\sum_{r\mid D}\gamma_{r}E_{r}(\tau)=\sum_{m\geq 0}B(m,0,s_{0})\cdot q^{m}

of forms in
M_{n}(\Gamma_{0}(D),\varepsilon^{n})

Here for a prime
\ell\mid D
we have set

\gamma_{\ell}=(D,\ell)_{\ell}^{n}\cdot\mathrm{inv}_{\ell}(V)\cdot\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }\ell\equiv 1\pmod{4}\\
i^{-n}&\mbox{if }\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4},\end{cases}

where
(-,-)_{\ell}
is the usual Hilbert symbol at
\ell
, and for a divisor
r\mid D
we have set

\gamma_{r}=\prod_{\ell\mid r}\gamma_{\ell}.

Proof.

We use the
\mathbb{C}
-bilinear pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
S_{L}
to write

\sum_{m\geq 0}B(m,0,s_{0})\cdot q^{m}=\langle E_{L},\varphi_{0}\rangle.

On the other hand, using (
2.4.4
) and the scalar Eisenstein series

E=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma_{0}(D)}\varepsilon^{n}(d)\cdot(1\mid_{n}\gamma)

of weight
n
for the cusp
\infty
in
M_{n}(\Gamma_{0}(D),\varepsilon^{n})
, we obtain

\displaystyle E_{L}=E_{L}(\tau,s_{0},n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}(c\tau+d)^{-n}\cdot\bar{\omega}_{L}(\gamma)^{-1}\varphi_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}(D)\backslash\Gamma}(E\mid_{n}\gamma)\cdot\bar{\omega}_{L}(\gamma)^{-1}\varphi_{0}.

Hence

\displaystyle\langle E_{L},\varphi_{0}\rangle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}(D)\backslash\Gamma}(E\mid_{n}\gamma)\cdot\langle\bar{\omega}_{L}(\gamma)^{-1}\varphi_{0},\varphi_{0}\rangle.

We compute this finite sum by means of the left coset decomposition

\Gamma=\bigsqcup_{r\mid D}\bigsqcup_{c\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma_{0}(D)R_{r}\begin{pmatrix}1&c\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

see e.g. equation (8.2.1) in
[
BHK+20a
]
. Using the notation of
[
BHK+20a
]
we find

\displaystyle\langle E_{L},\varphi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}(D)\backslash\Gamma}(E\mid_{n}\gamma)\cdot\langle\varphi_{0},\omega_{L}(\gamma)\varphi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\mid D}\sum_{c\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}(E\mid_{n}R_{r}\begin{pmatrix}1&c\\
0&1\end{pmatrix})\cdot\langle\varphi_{0},\omega_{L}(R_{r})\omega(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&c\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right))\varphi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\mid D}\sum_{c\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}}(E\mid_{n}W_{r}\begin{pmatrix}1&c\\
0&r\end{pmatrix})\cdot\langle\varphi_{0},\omega_{L}(R_{r})\varphi_{0}\rangle.

In terms of the
U_{r}
operators on
M_{n}(\Gamma_{0}(D),\varepsilon^{n})
,
given by
\sum_{n}a(n)q^{n}\mid_{n}U_{r}=\sum_{n}a(rn)q^{n}
, we may rewrite this as

\displaystyle\langle E_{L},\varphi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\mid D}\langle\varphi_{0},\omega_{L}(R_{r})\varphi_{0}\rangle\cdot r^{1-n/2}(E\mid_{n}W_{r}\mid_{n}U_{r}).

By means of equation (8.2.3) of
[
BHK+20a
]
, we obtain for the matrix coefficients

\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{0},\omega_{L}(R_{r})\varphi_{0}\rangle=\varepsilon_{r^{\prime}}^{n}(\alpha)\varepsilon_{r}^{n}(-\beta)\gamma_{r}r^{-n/2},

where we have written
rr^{\prime}=D
.
Inserting the definition

E_{r}=\varepsilon_{r}^{n}(-\beta)\varepsilon_{r^{\prime}}^{n}(\alpha r)(E\mid_{n}W_{r})

of the Eisenstein series for the cusp
\infty_{r}=r/D
, we find

\displaystyle\langle E_{L},\varphi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\mid D}\varepsilon_{r^{\prime}}^{n}(r)\gamma_{r}r^{1-n}(E_{r}\mid_{n}U_{r}).

It follows from the Fourier expansion of
E_{r}
given in Proposition
2.7.1
that

E_{r}\mid_{n}U_{r}=\varepsilon_{r^{\prime}}^{n}(r)r^{n-1}E_{r}.

Inserting this, we finally obtain

\displaystyle\langle E_{L},\varphi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\mid D}\gamma_{r}E_{r},

concluding the proof of the proposition.
∎

The following fact about the coefficients of the Eisenstein series of Proposition
2.7.1
will be needed much later, in the proof of Lemma
8.4.4
.

Proposition 2.7.4
.

For any positive
m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\gcd(m,D)=1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r\mid D}\gamma_{r}e_{r}(m)\log(D/r)=\left(\sum_{\ell\mid D}\frac{\log(\ell)}{1+\beta_{\ell}\cdot\left(\frac{m}{\ell}\right)^{n}}\right)\sum_{r\mid D}\gamma_{r}e_{r}(m).

Here both sums over
r\mid D
are over all positive divisors of
D
, the sum over
\ell\mid D
is over only the prime divisors,
\gamma_{r}
is as in Proposition
2.7.3
, and we abbreviate

\beta_{\ell}=(-1)^{n+1}\operatorname{inv}_{\ell}(V)\cdot\begin{cases}\left(\frac{-1}{\ell}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\ell^{\frac{n}{2}}&\mbox{if $n$ is even}\\[8.61108pt]
\left(\frac{-1}{\ell}\right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\ell^{\frac{n-1}{2}}&\mbox{if $n$ is odd.}\end{cases}.

Proof.

Directly from the formulas of Proposition
2.7.1
, one can see that there is a factorization

\gamma_{r}e_{r}(m)=c(m)f_{r}(m)

in which
c(m)
is some function independent of
r
, and

f_{r}(m)=\left(\frac{m}{r}\right)^{n}\cdot\prod_{\ell\mid r}\beta_{\ell}.

The relation
f_{r}(m)=\prod_{\ell\mid r}f_{\ell}(m)
implies the combinatorial identity

\sum_{r\mid D}f_{r}(m)\log(D/r)=\left(\sum_{\ell\mid D}\frac{\log(\ell)}{1+f_{\ell}(m)}\right)\sum_{r\mid D}f_{r}(m),

and multiplying both sides by
c(m)
proves the claim.
∎

Chapter 3
Automorphic Green functions

As in Chapter
2
, we fix a
{\bm{k}}
-hermitian space
V
of signature
(n-1,1)
with
n\geq 1
.
We recall the family of special divisors on the complex Shimura variety associated to the unitary group
\operatorname{U}(V)
, and derive a geometric variant of the Siegel-Weil formula.
Using this, we compute the integrals of automorphic Green functions for the special divisors.

3.1.
A complex Shimura variety

The fixed embedding
{\bm{k}}\subset\mathbb{C}
identifies
{\bm{k}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{C}
, and allows us to distinguish between the two orthogonal idempotents
\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}\in{\bm{k}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
, which we label in such a way that

(\alpha\otimes 1)\epsilon=(1\otimes\alpha)\epsilon,\qquad(\alpha\otimes 1)\overline{\epsilon}=(1\otimes\overline{\alpha})\overline{\epsilon}

for all
\alpha\in{\bm{k}}
.

Viewing
V(\mathbb{R})=V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
as
\mathbb{C}
-hermitian space of signature
(n-1,1)
, define a hermitian symmetric domain
1
1

1

We take this opportunity to point out a misstatement in (2.1.2) of
[
BHK+20a
]
: the word “planes” should be replaced by “lines” in the definition of the hermitian symmetric domain.

(3.1.1)

\mathcal{D}=\{\mbox{negative definite complex lines }z\subset V(\mathbb{R})\}.

The natural map

V(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow V(\mathbb{C})=\epsilon V(\mathbb{C})\oplus\overline{\epsilon}V(\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{proj}}\epsilon V(\mathbb{C})

is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism, and identifies

(3.1.2)

\mathcal{D}=\{z\in\mathbb{P}(\epsilon V(\mathbb{C})):[z,\overline{z}]<0\}.

Here we have endowed
V(\mathbb{C})=V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
with the
\mathbb{C}
-bilinear extension of the
\mathbb{Q}
-bilinear form
[-,-]
of (
2.1.1
), and
z\mapsto\overline{z}
is the complex conjugation on the second factor in the tensor product.
Under either interpretation, there is an evident action of the real points of

H=\operatorname{U}(V)

on
\mathcal{D}
by holomorphic automorphisms.

Fix an
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-lattice
L\subset V
on which the hermitian form is
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{k}}
-valued.
Choose a compact open subgroup
K\subset H(\mathbb{A}_{f})
that stabilizes
L
, and hence acts on the finite dimensional vector space
S_{L}=\mathbb{C}[L^{\prime}/L]
of §
2.4
.
This choice determines a complex Shimura variety

(3.1.3)

\mathrm{Sh}_{K}(H,\mathcal{D})=H(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\mathcal{D}\times H(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K,
